in order to relate the right-hand side of formula (
189
) to the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formulae we have to introduce the
analytic subring

(194)

\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}\subset\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})

defined as the image of the
analytic Stanley-Reisner subring
(depending only on the fan
\Sigma
)

SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma):={\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}/\sim_{SR}\ \ \subset\ {\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]/\sim_{SR}=:\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}

under the isomorphism
\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}\ \simeq\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
given on generators by
x_{\rho}\mapsto F_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
. Given an element
p(F_{\rho})\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
, with
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
a convergent power series, one gets for the restriction
i_{\sigma}^{*}
to a fixed point
x_{\sigma}
that

(195)

p(i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho})=i_{\sigma}^{*}(p(F_{\rho})).

Here, the convergent power series on the left side corresponds to the image of
p(x_{\rho})
under the evaluation homomorphism

{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\mapsto(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}}^{an})_{\mathbb{Q}}:\>x_{\rho}\mapsto i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho}\in H^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(pt;{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq M_{\mathbb{Q}}\>.

Remark 5.10
.

Regarding
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
as a fan, we have a corresponding (analytic) Stanley-Reisner ring

SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma):={\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\sigma(1)\}\ \ \subset\ {\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\sigma(1)]]=:\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)},

with an isomorphism
\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)}\ \simeq\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(U_{\sigma};{\mathbb{Q}})\overset{i_{\sigma}^{*}}{\simeq}\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(x_{\sigma};{\mathbb{Q}})
given on generators by
x_{\rho}\mapsto i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\sigma(1)
. Then the restriction map
\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}\to\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(U_{\sigma};{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
is induced from a corresponding restriction map
SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)\to SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)
, sending
x_{\rho}\mapsto 0
if
\rho\notin\sigma(1)
. We get the following factorization of the right hand side of (
195
):

i_{\sigma}^{*}:\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}\to\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(U_{\sigma};{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}\to\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(x_{\sigma};{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.1
and Remark
5.2
, we get by differentiation and convergence the following.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}[[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
be an infinite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a convergent (near zero) power series with rational coefficients
p(x)\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
. Then, in the above notations, we get for
z
small enough and away from the hyperplanes
\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle=0
for each ray
\rho\in\sigma(1)
of
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
the following formula:

(196)

\begin{split}p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}&=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle(1+y)\cdot i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle p(i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho}),z\rangle\\
&=\langle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{i_{\sigma}^{*}\left(e^{(1+y)\cdot c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(F_{\rho})\right)}{Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma})},z\rangle\\
&=\langle\int_{X}e^{(1+y)\cdot c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(F_{\rho}),z\rangle\>.\end{split}

If
p_{n}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]
is the corresponding truncation of
p
up to order
n
, then both sides of (
196
) applied to
p_{n}
converge for
n\to\infty
locally uniformly in these
z
to (
196
) applied to
p
.

Remark 5.12
.

By (
196
), the operator
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}
depends only on the equivalence class of
[p(x)]\in SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)
and not on the chosen convergent power series representative.

Remark 5.13
.

Assume in addition in Corollary
5.11
that
\langle\int_{X}e^{(1+y)\cdot c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(F_{\rho}),z\rangle

is a convergent power series in
z
near zero (for
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
a fixed parameter, e.g.,
y=0
). Then one gets as an application of Cauchy’s integral formula (see also
[
34
]
[page 27]),
that both sides of (
196
) applied to
p_{n}
converge for
n\to\infty
and
z
small locally uniformly to (
196
) applied to
p
. In particular, this limit commutes with finite order differentiations with respect to
z
(and
z
small enough).

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
be an infinite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a formal power series with rational coefficients
p(x)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
. Then for a polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have the following formula:

(197)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\left(\langle\int_{X}e^{(1+y)\cdot c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(F_{\rho}),z\rangle\right)_{|_{z=0}}\>,

where on the right hand side the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}
acts on a formal power series in
z
.

Proof.

Let first
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]
be a finite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a polynomial with rational coefficients

p(x)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]
. Then the result follows from applying the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}

to (
196
). Here we first need to assume that
z
is small enough and away from the hyperplanes
\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle=0
for each ray
\rho\in\sigma(1)
of
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
, since the localization formula is used; however, formula (
196
) then holds for all
z
small enough (and
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
fixed) by continuity, since the left hand side of (
196
) is analytic in
z
near zero by Remark
5.2
.
Moreover, by Corollary
5.3
the left hand side and therefore also the right hand side of (
197
) only depend on a truncation of
p
up to order
n+deg(f)
. Especially the left hand side is well defined and the stated equality holds for a formal power series with rational coefficients
p(x)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

By (
197
), the operator
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(z)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}
depends only on the equivalence class of
[p(x)]\in\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}
and not on the chosen formal power series representative.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.8
and Remark
5.10
, we get the following local version of Corollary
5.11
:

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}[[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
be an infinite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a convergent (near zero) power series with rational coefficients
p(x)\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
. Fix a vertex
v\in P
with tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
, and let

\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
be the corresponding cone.
Then, for
z
small enough and
-z\in Int(\sigma)
, we have the following formula:

(198)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\langle\frac{e^{v}\cdot\left(i_{\sigma}^{*}p(F_{\rho})\right)}{Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma})},z\rangle\>.

If
p_{n}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]
is the corresponding truncation of
p
up to order
n
, then both sides of (
198
) applied to
p_{n}
converge for
n\to\infty
locally uniformly in these
z
to (
198
) applied to
p
.

We also have the following.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
X=X_{\Sigma}
be a projective simplicial toric variety. For any class
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
, its equivariant Todd class is an element in the analytic cohomology ring of
X
, i.e.,

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}\subset\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Proof.

For
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, this follows from the explicit formula given in (
130
). More generally, this holds for sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}=\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{W}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\chi^{\widetilde{m}}})^{G}
as in the explicit formula (
138
). As in the proof of
[
13
]
[Cor.1.2], the equivariant Grothendieck group
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
is generated by classes of sheaves of the form
\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{W}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\chi^{\widetilde{m}}})^{G}
.
∎

Altogether, we get the following abstract Euler-Maclaurin formula coming from the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch theorem.

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a convergent power series
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
so that
p(F_{\rho})={\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
. Then

(199)

\begin{split}p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}&=\langle\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}),z\rangle\\
&=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>,\end{split}

as analytic functions in
z
with
z
small enough, and with
\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})\in(\Lambda^{an}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
the cohomological equivariant Euler characteristic of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Equation (
196
) for
y=0
can now be calculated as

\displaystyle p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\int_{X}e^{c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}{\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}]),z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}),z\rangle,

where the last equality follows from the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula (
83
). The second equality of (
199
) follows from (
71
) which uses the eigenspace decomposition, with the minus sign of (
199
) due to the appearance of
c(m)=-s(m)
in (
71
). Recall that we work with the identification
s:Sym_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}(M)\simeq H_{\mathbb{T}}^{*}(pt;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})=:(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
.
Finally, in the proof we first need to assume that
z
is small enough and away from the hyperplanes
\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle=0
for each ray
\rho\in\sigma(1)
of
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
, since the localization formula is used; however, formula (
199
) then holds for all
z
small enough, by Remark
5.13
.
∎

Evaluating (
199
) at
z=0
, we get the following generalized
volume formula
.

Corollary 5.19
.

In the notations of the previous theorem, we have

(200)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left({vol}\ {P(h)}\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\chi(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}),

with
{vol}\ {P(h)}=\int_{P(h)}dm
the volume of
P(h)
, and the Lebesgue measure normalized so that the unit cube in
M\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
has volume
1
.

Example 5.20
.

The classical volume formula
[
12
]
[Thm.2.15] corresponds to
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
for
p
given by the Todd operator
Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
of (
203
) below, with
\chi(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))=|P\cap M|
. ∎

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a formal power series
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
so that
p(F_{\rho})={\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then for a polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have:

(201)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot f(m)\>.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
5.14

by applying the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}
to the last term of formula (
199
),
seen as a formal power series in
z
.
∎

Let us finish this subsection with a local counterpart of Theorem
5.18
. Using Remark
5.10
, Corollary
5.16
and Proposition
4.13
, we get:

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a convergent power series
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
so that
p(F_{\rho})={\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
.
Fix a vertex
v\in P
with tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
, and let

\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
be the corresponding cone.
Then, for
z
small enough with
-z\in Int(\sigma)
, we have the following formula:

(202)

\begin{split}p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}&=\langle e^{v}\cdot{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}])_{x_{\sigma}},z\rangle\\
&=\langle e^{v}\cdot{\rm ch}^{\mathbb{T}}((\mathbb{S}\circ\chi^{\mathbb{T}}_{\sigma})({\mathcal{F}})),z\rangle\>.\end{split}

5.3.
Examples of Euler-Maclaurin formulae

In this subsection, we explain how various special cases of Corollary
5.21
and Proposition
5.22
yield old and new Euler-Maclaurin type formulae.

In the global context of Corollary
5.21
, let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. We fix a polynomial
g
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f(m)=g(m)\cdot e^{\langle m,z\rangle}
be a quasi-polynomial, with
z\in N_{\mathbb{C}}
small enough. In the context of a tangent cone at a vertex
v
of
P
as in Proposition
5.22
, we only use an exponential function
f(m)=e^{\langle m,z\rangle}
with
z\in N_{\mathbb{C}}
small enough and
-z\in Int(\sigma)
. In this local case,
p(x)
is restricted to the variables
F_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\sigma(1)
(as in Remark
5.10
), with
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
the cone corresponding to
v
. In the concrete formulas below, this amounts to using the cone
\sigma
as a fan instead of
\Sigma
, and the finite group
G_{\sigma}
instead of
G_{\Sigma}
.

Example 5.23
.

The first case we consider is the classical one of the
Euler-Maclaurin formula for the polytope
P
. Here we choose
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
and the infinite order differential operator

(203)

Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}\in\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\;|\;\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\>,

as dictated by formula (
130
) for
{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}(X)
. Then the equivariant Euler characteristic formula (
75
) yields the Euler-Maclaurin formula of Brion-Vergne
[
13
]
[Thm.4.5], extended here to a quasi-polynomial
f
:

(204)

Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in P\cap M}f(m)\>.

As explain before, the left-hand side of (
204
) only depends of the class of
Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
in the analytic Stanley-Reisner ring of
X
. For example, another such representative corresponds to the power series fitting with (the equivariant version of) Theorem
2.6
, expressing the Todd operator in terms of suitable
L
-class versions.
If
P
is a Delzant polytope, one recovers formula (
184
) of Khovanskii-Pukhlikov, fitting also with the equivariant Todd class of smooth projective toric varieties (e.g., obtained by setting
y=0
in Corollary
3.23
).

In the local case of the tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
of
P
at a vertex
v
, using (
191
) we get (see also
[
38
]
[Thm.1],
[
34
]
[eqn.(B.1)]):

(205)

\begin{split}Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=e^{\langle v,z\rangle}\cdot\sum_{m\in Tan(P,v)\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>.\end{split}

∎

Example 5.24
.

An Euler-Maclaurin formula for the
interior
of a simple lattice polytope
P
can be obtained similarly by using the
dual Todd operator

(206)

Todd^{\vee}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{a_{\rho}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}\in\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\;|\;\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\>,

corresponding to the sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}=\omega_{X}
and its dual Todd class
{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([\omega_{X}]_{\mathbb{T}})
of formula (
131
). In particular, by the equivariant Euler characteristic formula (
76
) one gets:

(207)

Todd^{\vee}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in{\rm Int}(P)\cap M}f(m)\>.

In the local case of the tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
of
P
at a vertex
v
, using (
192
) we get:

(208)

\begin{split}Todd^{\vee}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=e^{\langle v,z\rangle}\cdot\sum_{m\in Int(Tan(P,v))\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>.\end{split}

∎

More generally, one can prove an
Euler-Maclaurin formula for a polytope with some facets removed
, see also
[
34
]
[Prop.7.2] for the case of Delzant polytopes. Let
P
be a full-dimensional polytope with
r
facets
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
. For a subset
K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
P^{K}
be the set obtained from
P
by removing the facets
F_{i}
,
i\in K
. For example,
P^{\emptyset}=P
and
P^{\{1,\ldots,r\}}={\rm Int}(P)
. Let
\Sigma(1)=\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{r}\}
be the rays of the inner normal fan of
P
, and denote by
h=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})
a vector of real numbers indexed by the facets of
P
(i.e.,
h_{i}:=h_{\rho_{i}}
). Consider the following operator in
{\mathbb{Q}}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}\mid i=1,\ldots,r\}
:

(209)

Todd^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{i\in K}\frac{a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}\cdot\prod_{i\notin K}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}},

corresponding to formula (
139
) for the equivariant Todd class of
[{\mathcal{F}}]=mC_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X])
, with
U=X\setminus D_{K}
the open complement of the divisor
D_{K}=\bigcup_{i\in K}D_{i}
and
D_{i}:=D_{\rho_{i}}
. Moreover, by (
140
) and Corollary
3.15
for
y=0
, applied to
X
and
D_{K}
, one gets a corresponding equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula for
{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes mC_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X]))
, namely

\int_{X}{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes mC_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X]))=\sum_{m\in P^{K}\cap M}e^{s(m)}.

For its local counterpart, we use (
172
) for
y=0
.
With the above notations, together with (
192
), this then gives the following.

Theorem 5.25
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then:

(210)

Todd^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in P^{K}\cap M}f(m).

In the local case of the tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
of
P
at a vertex
v
, we get:

(211)

\begin{split}Todd^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=e^{\langle v,z\rangle}\cdot\sum_{m\in Tan(P,v)^{K}\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>.\end{split}

We next explain an
Euler-Maclaurin formula for the interior of a face of a simple lattice polytope
. Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and fix a face
E
of
P
. Let
\sigma:=\sigma_{E}
be the corresponding cone in the inner normal fan
\Sigma=\Sigma_{P}
of
P
, with
V_{\sigma}=V_{\sigma_{E}}=X_{E}
the closure of the orbit of
\sigma
in
X=X_{P}
. Denote by
i_{E}=i_{\sigma}:V_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X
the closed inclusion map. Then
V_{\sigma}
is a simplicial toric variety whose fan is
Star(\sigma)
, as defined in (
28
), which is built from cones
\tau\in\Sigma
that have
\sigma
as a face.
Recall that
{\mathbb{T}}=T_{N}
acts on
V_{\sigma}
via the morphism
T_{N}\to T_{N(\sigma)}
. Then we get, as in Corollary
3.25
and with the above notations, the following formula:

(212)

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([\omega_{V_{\sigma}}]_{\mathbb{T}})=i_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{a_{\rho}(g)\cdot F_{\rho}\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}\right).

Together with (
62
) and the projection formula, this gives the equivariant Todd class of
[{\mathcal{F}}]=[(i_{\sigma})_{*}\omega_{V_{\sigma}}]
:

(213)

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([(i_{\sigma})_{*}\omega_{V_{\sigma}}])=\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}F_{\rho}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{a_{\rho}(g)\cdot F_{\rho}\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}.

Using (
120
), this yields the following result (where we use the above notations):

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for a fixed face
E
of
P
, with corresponding cone
\sigma
in the inner normal fan
\Sigma
of
P
, one has:

(214)

Todd^{\vee}_{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f(m),

where

(215)

Todd^{\vee}_{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{a_{\rho}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}.

We next discuss a similar
Euler-Maclaurin formula for a face of a simple lattice polytope
. With the same notations, we have by (
130
)
the following formula:

(216)

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\sigma}}]_{\mathbb{T}})=i_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}\right).

As before, the projection formula yields the equivariant Todd class of
[{\mathcal{F}}]=[(i_{\sigma})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\sigma}}]
:

(217)

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([(i_{\sigma})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\sigma}}])=\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}F_{\rho}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}.

Using (
121
), this yields the following result:

Theorem 5.27
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for a fixed face
E
of
P
, with corresponding cone
\sigma
in the inner normal fan
\Sigma
of
P
, one has:

(218)

Todd_{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in E\cap M}f(m),

where

(219)

Todd_{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}.

Other interesting coherent sheaves to consider are the Zariski sheaves
\widehat{\Omega}^{p}_{X}
of
p
-forms on the toric variety
X
. In the next section, these will be considered all at once via the formal sum

\bigoplus_{p}[\widehat{\Omega}^{p}_{X}]_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot y^{p}\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)[y],

and similarly for suitable motivic Chern classes
mC^{\mathbb{T}}_{y}
of
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant constructible subsets of
X
.

We leave it to the reader to specialize the generalized volume formula (
200
) to all situations discussed in this section.

Another way to obtain examples of explicit Euler-Maclaurin formulae is by twisting the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
by
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime}-D)
, for
D=D_{P}
the original ample divisor associated to the full-dimensional lattice polytope, and
D^{\prime}
any
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor on
X
. By the multiplicativity of the equivariant Todd class transformation for the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime}-D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}
, we have

{\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}])=e^{[D^{\prime}-D]_{\mathbb{T}}}\cdot{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}]).

So, if
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
is a convergent power series with
p(F_{\rho})=td_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}])
, then
p^{\prime}(x_{\rho}):=e^{\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}x_{\rho}}\cdot p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
is a convergent power series with
p^{\prime}(F_{\rho})=td_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}])
, where
D^{\prime}-D=\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}D_{\rho}
as a
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor.

Example 5.28
.

As a last concrete example of this section, assume
D^{\prime}
is a
globally generated

{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor on
X
, with associated (not necessarily full-dimensional) lattice polytope
P_{D^{\prime}}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Consider the infinite order differential operator

(220)

Todd^{\prime}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=e^{\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\cdot Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})

with
Todd(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
as in (
203
) and
d_{\rho}
’s as above.
Then the equivariant Euler characteristic formula (
79
) yields the following new Euler-Maclaurin formula for a quasi-polynomial
f
.

(221)

Todd^{\prime}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in P_{D^{\prime}}\cap M}f(m)\>.

Note that
P_{D^{\prime}}
is an
{\mathbb{N}}
-Minkowski summand (in the sense of
[
16
]
[Def.6.2.11]) of the original polytope
P
, and any such
{\mathbb{N}}
-Minkowski summand comes from a globally generated Cartier divisor
D^{\prime}
, see
[
16
]
[Cor.6.2.15].
∎

6.
Weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae

In this section we discuss several weighted (or parametrized) Euler-Maclaurin formulae, generalizing the weighted lattice point counting. For this purpose, we explain in Subsection
6.1
the use of an additional dilation parameter
y
fitting with the equivariant Hirzebruch classes and their renormalization. In Subsection
6.2
, we specialize the parametrized version of the Euler-Maclaurin formula to various situations, recovering some known weighted Euler-Maclaurin type formulae from
[
5
,
30
]
, but also obtaining several new ones in a uniform way dictated by toric geometry. For instance, we get weighted Euler-Maclaurin type formulae for simple polytopes possibly with some facets deleted (e.g., for the interior of a polytope). Similarly, we obtain such weighted formulae for (interiors of) faces of simple polytopes, as well as for
{\mathbb{N}}
-Minkowski summands of simple polytopes corresponding to globally generated
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisors on the corresponding toric variety.

6.1.
Abstract weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
.

Let a convergent power series
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
be given. Additionally, we may start with a polynomial in
y
with coefficients consisting of such power series, as we will need in the applications of this section. Define a corresponding
renormalized series

p_{y}(x_{\rho}):=\frac{p\left((1+y)x_{\rho}\right)}{(1+y)^{n}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[y,(1+y)^{-1}][[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]].

To treat it as a convergent power series, one needs to assume that
y\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{1\}
is fixed or it belongs to a compact subset.

Let
p_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
be the corresponding parametrized infinite order differential operator obtained from
p_{y}(x_{\rho})
by substituting
x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
, for all
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.
Then formula (
196
) translates into the following:

(222)

p_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle(1+y)\cdot i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P})),z\rangle}}{(1+y)^{n}\cdot\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle p((1+y)\cdot i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho}),z\rangle\>.

Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a convergent power series
p(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
so that
p(F_{\rho})={\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
. Applying the proof of Theorem
5.18
to formula (
222
), one then gets

(223)

p_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot e^{\langle(1+y)m,z\rangle}\>.

Here the use of the renormalized power series
p_{y}
and polytope
P_{y}(h)
correspond to multiplying each degree
2k
equivariant cohomology class by
(1+y)^{k}
. (This is just the cohomological Adams operation
\Psi^{(1+y)}
.) In particular, via the identification
s:M\simeq H^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(pt;{\mathbb{Z}})
,

m\in M
gets multiplied by
1+y
.

For any polynomial
f
defined on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, by applying the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}
to formula (
223
), we get the following
parametrized Euler-Maclaurin formula
:

(224)

p_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot f\left((1+y)m\right)\>.

Moreover, if
f
is homogeneous of degree
\deg(f)
, then

f\left((1+y)m\right)=(1+y)^{\deg(f)}\cdot f(m).

Remark 6.1
.

In the local case of Euler-Maclaurin formulae for a tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
, we only work with the unnormalized equivariant Hirzebruch classes
T^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}
and the dilation
Tan(P,v)(h)
of the tangent cone. In this context, a renormalization is not needed.

6.2.
Examples of weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae

In this subsection, we apply the above renormalization not just to classes of coherent sheaves, but directly to examples of the type

\bigoplus_{p}[\widehat{\Omega}^{p}_{X}]_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot y^{p}\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)[y],

as well as for suitable motivic Chern classes
mC^{\mathbb{T}}_{y}
of
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant constructible subsets of
X
.

Recall from Subsection
3.3
that the equivariant Hirzebruch classes
{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})[y]
, resp.,
\widehat{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}[y])
of
X
are computed as:

(225)

{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}\>,

(226)

\widehat{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}(1+y)}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}(1+y)}}\>,

with
r=|\Sigma(1)|
, and
F_{\rho}=[D_{\rho}]_{\mathbb{T}}
denoting the equivariant fundamental class of the
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant divisor
D_{\rho}
corresponding to the ray
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.

The weighted lattice point counting of Theorem
2.3
(and Remark
2.4
), coupled with the above expressions for the equivariant Hirzebruch classes, suggest that a weighted Euler-Maclaurin-type formula can be computed by using the Hirzebruch (or, parametrized Todd) differential operators defined by:

{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}

and

\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}(1+y)}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}(1+y)}}.

The passage from
{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
to
\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
is just a special case of our general procedure of
moving from
p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
to
p_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
. This therefore fits with the renormalization of Hirzebruch classes, from
{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)
to
\widehat{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)
.

Weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae were considered from a combinatorial point of view in
[
30
]
[Thm.1.1 and Rem.1.2] for
{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
, and more recently in
[
5
]
[Thm.1.5] for
\widehat{T}^{\mathbb{T}}_{y*}(X)
.
We reprove here
the following result (see
[
30
]
[Thm.1.1 and Rem.1.2] for
{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
, and
[
5
]
[Thm.4.6] for
\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
), from the point of view of generalized Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula:

Theorem 6.2
.

For
z\in N_{\mathbb{C}}
small enough, one has in the above notations:

(227)

{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq P}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}.

(228)

\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq P}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}e^{\langle(1+y)m,z\rangle}.

In the corresponding local case of the tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
of
P
at a vertex
v
, for
z\in N_{\mathbb{C}}
small enough with
-z\in Int(\sigma)
,we get

(229)

\begin{split}T_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq Tan(P,v)}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\cdot e^{\langle v,z\rangle}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>.\end{split}

Proof.

To deduce formula (
227
), instead of
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
one considers the motivic Chern class
mC^{\mathbb{T}}_{y}(X)\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)[y]
, with

T_{y*}(X):={\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}(mC^{\mathbb{T}}_{y}(X))
. We work with the power series
p(x_{\rho}):=T_{y}(x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}[y]
fitting with the differential operator
{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
. Formula (
227
) follows now from (
199
) (linearly extended in
y
), using the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula of Corollary
3.15
, applied to the case
P^{\prime}=P
. Formula (
228
) is then obtained via renormalization using (
223
).

For the local result, we use (
166
) together with (
192
).
∎

Using (
224
), one then obtains the following result (see also
[
30
]
[Thm.1.1 and Rem.1.2] and
[
5
]
[Thm.1.5]):

Corollary 6.3
.

For any polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, one has:

(230)

{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq P}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f(m).

(231)

\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq P}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f((1+y)m).

Note that the Brion-Verne Euler-Maclaurin formula for the simple lattice polytope
P
is obtained from either (
230
) or (
231
) by specializing to
y=0
. Moreover, for
y=1
, one gets Euler-Maclaurin formulas corresponding to operators related to suitable
L
-classes, i.e.,
\widehat{T}_{1*}(X)=L_{*}(X)
the Thom-Milnor
L
-class of
X
(see
[
36
]
). Taking the top degree in
y
on both sides of (
230
) recovers formula (
207
).

Next we prove a
weighted Euler-Maclaurin formula for a polytope with some facets removed
. Here and below, for simplicity, we only indicate such a formula for a polynomial
f
, while we leave it to the reader to formulate the corresponding exponential formula. Let
P
be a full-dimensional polytope with
r
facets
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
. For a subset
K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
P^{K}
be as before the set obtained from
P
by removing the facets
F_{i}
,
i\in K
. So
P^{\emptyset}=P
and
P^{\{1,\ldots,r\}}={\rm Int}(P)
. Let
\Sigma(1)=\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{r}\}
be the rays of the inner normal fan of
P
, and denote by
h=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})
a vector of real numbers indexed by the facets of
P
(i.e.,
h_{i}:=h_{\rho_{i}}
). Consider the following operator in
{\mathbb{Q}}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}\mid i=1,\ldots,r\}[y]
:

(232)

T_{y}^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{i\in K}\frac{(1+y)\cdot a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}\cdot\prod_{i\notin K}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}(1+y\cdot a_{\rho_{i}}(g)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}})}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}},

corresponding to formula (
136
) for the equivariant Todd class of
mC_{y}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X])
, with
U=X\setminus D_{K}
the open complement of the divisor
D_{K}=\bigcup_{i\in K}D_{i}
and
D_{i}:=D_{\rho_{i}}
. By additivity,

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}(mC_{y}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X]))=T_{y*}([U\hookrightarrow X])=T_{y*}(X)-T_{y*}(D_{K})

By Corollary
3.15
, applied to
X
and
D_{K}
, one gets a corresponding equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula:

(233)

\int_{X}{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes mC_{y}^{\mathbb{T}}([U\hookrightarrow X]))=\sum_{E\preceq P^{K}}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}e^{s(m)}.

With the above notations this then gives the following.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then:

(234)

T_{y}^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\ \sum_{E\preceq P^{K}}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f(m).

By renormalization,
T_{y}^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
changes to

\widehat{T}_{y}^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{\Sigma}}\prod_{i\in K}\frac{(1+y)\cdot a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}\cdot e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}\cdot\prod_{i\notin K}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}(1+y\cdot a_{\rho_{i}}(g)e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}})}{1-a_{\rho_{i}}(g)\cdot e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{i}}}}

fitting with formula (
137
). We thus get by (
224
) the following

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then:

(235)

\widehat{T}_{y}^{K}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\ \sum_{E\preceq P^{K}}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f((1+y)m).

Remark 6.6
.

In the corresponding local case of the tangent cone
Tan(P,v)
of
P
at a vertex
v
, for
z\in N_{\mathbb{C}}
small enough with
-z\in Int(\sigma)
, we get by (
155
), (
157
) (
172
), together with (
192
), the following:

(236)

\begin{split}T^{K}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{Tan(P,v)(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E\preceq Tan(P,v)^{K}}(1+y)^{\dim(E)}\cdot e^{\langle v,z\rangle}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>.\end{split}

We next discuss a
weighted Euler-Maclaurin formula for a face of a simple lattice polytope
.
Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and fix a face
E
of
P
. Let
\sigma:=\sigma_{E}
be the corresponding cone in the inner normal fan
\Sigma=\Sigma_{P}
of
P
, with
V_{\sigma}=V_{\sigma_{E}}=X_{E}
the closure of the orbit of
\sigma
in
X=X_{P}
. Denote by
i_{E}=i_{\sigma}:V_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X
the closed inclusion map. Then
V_{\sigma}
is a simplicial toric variety whose fan is
Star(\sigma)
, as defined in (
28
), which is built from cones
\tau\in\Sigma
that have
\sigma
as a face.
Recall that
{\mathbb{T}}=T_{N}
acts on
V_{\sigma}
via the morphism
T_{N}\to T_{N(\sigma)}
. We have by (
225
)
the following formula:

(237)

T_{y*}^{\mathbb{T}}([id_{V_{\sigma}}])=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot i_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}\right).

The projection formula then yields the computation of the following equivariant Hirzebruch class:

(238)

T_{y*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{V_{\sigma}}\hookrightarrow X])=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}F_{\rho}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{F_{\rho}\cdot\big(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}\big)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-F_{\rho}}}.

Using Corollary
3.15
, this yields the following result:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
P
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
f
be a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, for a fixed face
E
of
P
, with corresponding cone
\sigma
in the inner normal fan
\Sigma
of
P
, one has:

(239)

T_{y}^{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E^{\prime}\preceq E}(1+y)^{\dim(E^{\prime})}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E^{\prime})\cap M}f(m),

where

(240)

T_{y}^{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=(1+y)^{n-r}\cdot\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\left(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\right)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}.

For
y=0
, formula (
239
) specializes to (
218
).
Taking the top degree in
y
on both sides of (
239
) gives back formula (
214
).

By renormalization,
T_{y}^{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
changes to

\widehat{T}_{y}^{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=\sum_{g\in G_{Star(\sigma)}}{\rm mult}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in Star(\sigma)(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\left(1+y\cdot a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\right)}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-(1+y)\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}.

We thus get by (
224
) the following

Corollary 6.8
.

In the above notations, we get

(241)

\widehat{T}_{y}^{E}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{E^{\prime}\preceq E}(1+y)^{\dim(E^{\prime})}\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E^{\prime})\cap M}f((1+y)m).

For
y=1
, one gets Euler-Maclaurin formulas corresponding to operators related to suitable
L
-classes, i.e.,
\widehat{T}_{1*}(X_{E})=L_{*}(X_{E})
the Thom-Milnor
L
-class of
X_{E}
.

Another way to obtain examples of explicit weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae is by twisting the equivariant motivic Chern class
mC_{y}^{\mathbb{T}}
by sheaves of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime}-D)
, for
D=D_{P}
the original ample divisor associated to the full-dimensional lattice polytope, and
D^{\prime}
any
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor on
X
.
In the next example, we illustrate this principle in the case of
mC_{y}^{\mathbb{T}}(X)
, corresponding to Theorem
6.2
and Corollary
6.3
.

Example 6.9
.

Let
D^{\prime}
be a globally generated
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor on
X
, with associated (not necessarily full-dimensional) lattice polytope
P_{D^{\prime}}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Consider
D^{\prime}-D=\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}D_{\rho}
as a
{\mathbb{T}}
-invariant Cartier divisor.
Let
X_{D^{\prime}}
be the toric variety of the lattice polytope
P_{D^{\prime}}
, defined via the corresponding generalized fan as in
[
16
]
[Prop.6.2.3]. Then, by
[
16
]
[Thm.6.2.8], there is a proper toric morphism
f:X\to X_{D^{\prime}}
, induced by the corresponding lattice projection
N\to N_{D^{\prime}}
given by dividing out by the minimal cone of the generalized fan of
P_{D^{\prime}}
. In particular,
f:X\to X_{D^{\prime}}
is a toric fibration. For
\sigma^{\prime}
a cone in the generalized fan of
P_{D^{\prime}}
, define as in (
106
)

d_{\ell}(X/\sigma^{\prime}):=|\Sigma_{\ell}(X/\sigma^{\prime})|

with

\Sigma_{\ell}(X/\sigma^{\prime}):=\{\sigma\in\Sigma\mid O_{\sigma}\subset X,\ f(O_{\sigma})=O_{\sigma^{\prime}},\ \ell=\dim(O_{\sigma})-\dim(O_{\sigma^{\prime}})\}.

Consider the infinite order differential operators

(242)

T^{\prime}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=e^{\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\cdot T_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})

and

(243)

\widehat{T}_{y}^{\prime}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}):=e^{(1+y)\cdot\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}d_{\rho}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}\cdot\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}),

with
T_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h}),\widehat{T}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})
as in Theorem
6.2
and Corollary
6.3
. For any quasi-polynomial
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, one then has by (
122
) the following new weighted Euler-Maclaurin formulae:

(244)

T^{\prime}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\\
=\sum_{E\preceq P_{D^{\prime}}}\left(\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(-1)^{\ell}\cdot d_{\ell}(X/E)\cdot(1+y)^{\ell+\dim(E)}\right)\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f(m).

(245)

\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{y}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P_{y}(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\\
=\sum_{E\preceq P_{D^{\prime}}}\left(\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(-1)^{\ell}\cdot d_{\ell}(X/E)\cdot(1+y)^{\ell+\dim(E)}\right)\cdot\sum_{m\in{\rm Relint}(E)\cap M}f((1+y)m).

As noted in Example
5.28
,
P_{D^{\prime}}
is an
{\mathbb{N}}
-Minkowski summand (in the sense of
[
16
]
[Def.6.2.11]) of the original polytope
P
, see
[
16
]
[Cor.6.2.15].
∎

7.
Euler-Maclaurin formulae via the Cappell-Shaneson algebra

In this section we provide generalizations of Cappell-Shaneson’s Euler-Maclaurin formula
[
18
,
43
]
and explain its connection to equivariant toric geometry. These Euler-Maclaurin formulae of Cappell-Shaneson type do not use a dilation of the lattice polytope, but a summation of integrals over the faces.

In Subsection
7.1
we recall the classical Euler-Maclaurin formula of Cappell-Shaneson
[
18
,
43
]
, formulated in terms of the so-called Cappell-Shaneson algebra (cf.
[
34
]
), and explain the identification of this algebra with the completed equivariant cohomology ring of the associated simplicial toric variety (regarded as an algebra of the completed equivariant cohomology ring of a point space).

In Subsection
7.2
, we prove an abstract Euler-Maclaurin formula of Cappell-Shaneson type, again for arbitrary
{\mathbb{T}}
-equivariant coherent sheaf coefficients (with the classical case corresponding to the structure sheaf). The key ingredient in the proof is a Stokes’ type formula which bridges integrals over the dilated polytopes and their faces. As before, we specialize this abstract formula to contexts dictated by various natural choices of coefficients attached to the toric variety.

In the final Subsection
7.3
, we indicate an application of our abstract Euler-Maclaurin formula to generalized reciprocity for Dedekind sums.

7.1.
Cappell-Shaneson algebra vs. completed equivariant cohomology ring

Let
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Instead of using a dilation
P(h)
of the polytope
P
, the Euler-Maclaurin formula of Cappell-Shaneson
[
18
,
43
]
uses a summation of integrals over the faces
E
of
P
(compare also with
[
14
]
[Sect.3.7]). More precisely, for any polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
one has:

(246)

\sum_{m\in P\cap M}\;f(m)=\sum_{E\preceq P}\>\int_{E}p_{\sigma_{E}}(\partial_{i})f(m)dm\>,

with
p_{\sigma_{E}}(\partial_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}]]
suitable infinite order differential operators with constant rational coefficients
in the partial derivatives with respect to the coordinates of the vector space
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Here the Lebesgue measure
dm
on
E
is normalized so that the unit cube in the lattice
Span(E_{0})\cap M
has volume
1
, with
E_{0}:=E-m_{0}
a translation of
E
by a vertex
m_{0}\in E
.

The infinite order differential operators
p_{\sigma_{E}}(\partial_{i})
are defined through some relations
in what is called in
[
34
]
[Sect.6] the
Cappell-Shaneson algebra
of
P
:

{\mathcal{A}}(P):=\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}]][U_{F}\mid\text{$F$ a facet of $P$}]/\sim

with relations

(247)

U_{F_{1}}\cdots U_{F_{k}}=0\quad\text{for distinct $F_{i}$ with \ $F_{1}\cap\dots\cap F_{k}=\emptyset$},

and

(248)

\frac{\partial}{\partial m}+\sum_{F}\;\langle m,n_{F}\rangle U_{F}=0\quad\text{for all $m\in M$ (or a basis of $M$).}

Here
\frac{\partial}{\partial m}
is the differentiation in the direction of
m
, with
n_{F}\in M
the minimal lattice vector orthogonal to
F
and pointing into
P
.
Let us rewrite this presentation of the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}(P)
(given above in terms of the polytope
P
) into the language of this paper using another description of the completed Stanley-Reisner ring

{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]/\sim_{SR}=:\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}

of the associated toric variety
X_{P}
and the inner normal fan
\Sigma=\Sigma_{P}
. Note that the inner normal vector
n_{F}
corresponds to the generator
u_{\rho}
of the ray
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
corresponding to
F
.
For simplicity, we fix a basis
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
of
M\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Z}$}^{n}
, with
t_{i}
the corresponding coordinates on
M_{\mathbb{K}}
(for
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{R}}
) with respect to this basis, so that
\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}=\partial_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then

\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n},U_{F}\mid\text{$F$ a facet of $P$}]

via
t_{i}\mapsto\partial_{i}
and
x_{\rho}\mapsto U_{F}
, with
F
the facet of
P
corresponding to
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.
The relation (
247
) corresponds to the Stanly-Reisner relation
\sim_{SR}
, and the relation
(
248
) translates into

(249)

t_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\;\langle m_{i},n_{\rho}\rangle x_{\rho}=0\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,n$.}

Denote by
\sim
both relations together on
\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]
.
These are also homogeneous for the usual grading doubled (i.e., with
x_{\rho}
and
t_{i}
of degree two). Then one gets
(see also
[
28
]
[Lecture13, Lem.2.2]):

Lemma 7.1
.

The inclusion
\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]\hookrightarrow\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]

induces an isomorphism of graded rings

SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)=\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]/\sim_{SR}\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]/\sim

and their formal power series completions

\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}=\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]]/\sim_{SR}\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]]/\sim\>,

with the analytic Stanley-Reisner subring
SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)=\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}\{x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}\}/\sim_{SR}
identified with the image of the projection from
\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}\}
.

Proof.

Using the invertible variable transformation

x_{\rho}\mapsto x_{\rho},\ t_{i}\mapsto t^{\prime}_{i}:=t_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\;\langle m_{i},n_{\rho}\rangle x_{\rho}

(for
i=1,\dots,n
and
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
),
the claim is reduced to the corresponding inclusion

\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]\hookrightarrow\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t^{\prime}_{1},\dots,t^{\prime}_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]\>,

with
\sim
given by
\sim_{SR}
and
t^{\prime}_{i}=0
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
In this case the results are obvious.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

Using the isomorphism

s:Sym_{\mathbb{Q}}(M)\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}=H_{\mathbb{T}}^{*}(pt;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$});\ \ m\mapsto-c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\chi^{m}})

together with
{\mathbb{Q}}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]=Sym_{\mathbb{Q}}(M)
, one gets isomorphisms of
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
-algebras

SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]/\sim\>\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]/\sim\>\simeq H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})

and

\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]]/\sim\>\simeq(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}[[x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]]/\sim\>\simeq\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\>,

with
t_{i}\mapsto s(m_{i})=-c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\chi^{m_{i}}})
for
i=i,\dots,n
, and

x_{\rho}\mapsto F_{\rho}=[D_{\rho}]_{\mathbb{T}}=c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{O}(D_{\rho}))
for
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.

Proof.

It suffices to note that the relation (
249
) corresponds to (
67
).
∎

Remark 7.3
.

Let
P\subset M_{{\mathbb{R}}}
be a simple full-dimensional lattice polytope with associated projective toric variety
X=X_{P}
and the inner normal fan

\Sigma=\Sigma_{P}
. Fix a basis
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
of
M\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Z}$}^{n}
. There is an algebra homomorphism
from the Cappell-Shaneson algebra
{\mathcal{A}}(P)
to the completed equivariant cohomology ring

(250)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{A}}(P)&=\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}]][U_{F}]/\sim\>\to\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}\simeq\ \widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$});\>\\
&U_{F}\mapsto x_{\rho}\simeq F_{\rho}=[D_{\rho}]_{\mathbb{T}}=c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{O}(D_{\rho}))\end{split}

as a
\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}]]\simeq\widehat{H}_{\mathbb{T}}^{*}(pt;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})\simeq(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
-algebra by

\partial_{i}\simeq t_{i}\simeq s(m_{i})=-c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}(\chi^{m_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
and with the facet
F
of
P
corresponding to the ray
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.
As we shall now explain, the algebra map (
250
) is in fact an isomorphism.
In abstract terms, the completions
\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}\simeq\ \widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})
are completions of connected integer graded commutative rings
R^{*}
with respect to the maximal ideal
R^{>0}
given by positive degree elements, whereas
{\mathcal{A}}(P)
uses the completion with respect to the maximal ideal
I_{\mathbb{T}}
of
\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n}]\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
. As
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}[U_{F}]
is not a finitely generated
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
-module, the completion functor with respect to
I_{\mathbb{T}}
is only left exact. In particular, there is an injective algebra homomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}(P)\hookrightarrow\left(\widehat{H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})}\right)_{I_{\mathbb{T}}}
, which factorizes (
250
).
Using the relations defining
{\mathcal{A}}(P)
, one gets as in
[
18
]
[Proposition on pag. 888] or
[
43
]
[6.1, pag. 621] that the image of this monomorphism is the
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
-submodule generated by the equivalence classes
[V_{E}]\in{\mathcal{A}}(P)
associated to all faces
E
of
P
. This also shows that one gets a natural algebra homomorphism
{\mathbb{Q}}[[U_{F}]]\to{\mathcal{A}}(P)
inducing algebra homomorphisms

\widehat{H}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\to{\mathcal{A}}(P)\to(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}[[x_{\rho}]]/\sim.

Since this composition is an isomorphism, the first arrow is injective and the second is surjective.
Using the fact that
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{*}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
, one also has that
\left(\widehat{H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})}\right)_{I_{\mathbb{T}}}\simeq(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{*}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is also generated as a
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
-module by the equivariant fundamental classes of the orbit closures
[V_{\sigma_{E}}]_{\mathbb{T}}
associated to all faces
E
of
P
(since
H^{*}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
is generated as a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector space by the fundamental classes
[V_{\sigma_{E}}]
). This shows that
{\mathcal{A}}(P)\simeq\left(\widehat{H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})}\right)_{I_{\mathbb{T}}}
. Finally, the natural algebra homomorphism

\left(\widehat{H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})}\right)_{I_{\mathbb{T}}}\to\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$})
is an isomorphism by
[
10
]
[Prop.1.4]. Therefore, the homomorphism (
250
) is an isomorphism.

7.2.
Euler-Maclaurin formulae via the Cappell-Shaneson algebra

In this subsection we provide a new proof of the Euler-Maclaurin formula of Cappell-Shaneson
[
18
,
43
]
, as well as several generalizations.

Let us now introduce the key functionals following
[
34
]
[Section 6].

Definition 7.4
.

Let

p(t_{i},x_{\rho}):=\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>p_{\alpha}(x_{\rho})\prod_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}\},

resp.,
{\mathbb{Q}}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]]

be a convergent, resp., formal power series in the
t_{i},x_{\rho}
. Then

(251)

p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}:=\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>p_{\alpha}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}

resp.,

(252)

p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}:=\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>p_{\alpha}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}

for
f
a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Note that in the second case in the last term only finitely many summands are non-zero due to Corollary
5.3
.

Of course in (
251
) one has to explain in which sense the series is convergent, as will be discussed in the following.
As a consequence of Theorem
5.1
(with
y=0
), Remark
5.2
and Corollary
5.11
, we get by differentiation and convergence the following.

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n},\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
be an infinite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
t_{i}\mapsto\partial_{i},x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a convergent power series with rational coefficients
p(t_{i},x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
. Then, in the above notations, we get for
z
small enough and away from the hyperplanes
\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle=0
for each ray
\rho\in\sigma(1)
of
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
the following formula:

(253)

\begin{split}&p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>p_{\alpha}(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}\\
&=\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>\prod_{i=1}^{n}\langle m_{i},z\rangle^{\alpha_{i}}\left(\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle p_{\alpha}(i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho}),z\rangle\right)\\
&=\langle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{i_{\sigma}^{*}\left(e^{(c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}\left(\sum_{\alpha=(\alpha_{i})\in\mbox{$\mathbb{N}$}_{0}^{n}}\>\prod_{i=1}^{n}s(m_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}p_{\alpha}(F_{\rho})\right)\right)}{Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma})},z\rangle\\
&=\langle\int_{X}e^{(c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho}),z\rangle\>.\end{split}

If
p_{n}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n},\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]
is the corresponding truncation of
p
up to order
n
, then both sides of (
253
) applied to
p_{n}
converge for
n\to\infty
locally uniformly in these
z
to (
253
) applied to
p
.

Proof.

The second equality follows from
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\langle m_{i},z\rangle^{\alpha_{i}}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}
and Corollary
5.11
.
The third equality uses
m_{i}=i_{\sigma}^{*}s(m_{i})\in M\simeq H^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(pt;{\mathbb{Q}})
for
s(m_{i})\in H^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
.
The last equality follows from equation (
177
), where the element
p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho})
corresponds to the image of
p(t_{i},x_{\rho})
under the evaluation homomorphism

{\mathbb{Q}}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\mapsto\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}:\>t_{i}\mapsto s(m_{i}),x_{\rho}\mapsto F_{\rho}\>.

∎

Remark 7.6
.

By (
253
), the operator
p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}
depends only on the equivalence class of
[p(t_{i},x_{\rho})]\in SR^{an}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)\simeq\mbox{$\mathbb{Q}$}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\text{$\rho\in\Sigma(1)$}]\}/\sim
and not on the chosen convergent power series representative.

Remark 7.7
.

Assume in addition in Corollary
7.5
, that
\langle\int_{X}e^{(c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho}),z\rangle

is a convergent power series in
z
near zero. Then one gets as an application of Cauchy’s integral formula (see also
[
34
]
[p.27]),
that both sides of (
253
) applied to
p_{n}
converge for
n\to\infty
and
z
small locally uniformly to (
253
) applied to
p
. In particular, this limit commutes with finite order differentiations with respect to
z
(and
z
small enough).

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n},\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
be an infinite order differential operator with constant rational coefficients, i.e., obtained by substituting
t_{i}\mapsto\partial_{i},x_{\rho}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}
into a formal power series with rational coefficients
p(t_{i},x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
. Then for a polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have the following formula:

(254)

p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\left(\langle\int_{X}e^{c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho}),z\rangle\right)_{|_{z=0}}\>,

where on the right hand side the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}
acts on a formal power series in
z
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Corollary
5.14
.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

By (
254
), the operator
p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(z)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}
depends only on the equivalence class of
[p(t_{i},x_{\rho})]\in\widehat{SR_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)}
and not on the chosen formal power series representative.

Altogether, we get the following second abstract Euler-Maclaurin formula coming from the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch theorem.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a convergent power series
p(t_{i},x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}
so that
p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho})={\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\left(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)^{an}
. Then

(255)

\begin{split}p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}&=\langle\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}),z\rangle\\
&=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\>,\end{split}

as analytic functions in
z
with
z
small enough, and with
\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})\in(\Lambda^{an}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
the cohomological equivariant Euler characteristic of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Equation (
253
) can now be calculated as

\displaystyle p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\int_{X}e^{c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D))}{\rm td}^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}]),z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\chi^{\mathbb{T}}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}),z\rangle,

where the last equality follows from the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula (
83
) as in the proof of Theorem
5.18
.
Finally, in the proof we first need to assume that
z
is small enough and away from the hyperplanes
\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle=0
for each ray
\rho\in\sigma(1)
of
\sigma\in\Sigma(n)
, since the localization formula is used; however, formula (
255
) then holds for all
z
small enough, by Remark
7.7
.
∎

Note that Theorem
7.10
reduces to Theorem
5.18
in the case when

p(t_{i},x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}\{x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)\}

does not depend on the variables
t_{i}
. Similarly, the next Corollary reduces in this case to Corollary
5.21
.

Corollary 7.11
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose a formal power series
p(t_{i},x_{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]
so that
p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho})=td^{\mathbb{T}}_{*}([{\mathcal{F}}])\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then for a polynomial function
f
on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have:

(256)

p(\partial_{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}=\sum_{m\in M}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{i}(X;{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)\otimes{\mathcal{F}})_{\chi^{-m}}\right)\cdot f(m)\>.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
7.8
by applying the operator
\left(f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})\right)_{|_{z=0}}
to the last term of formula (
255
), viewed as a formal power series in
z
.
∎

For the use of integration over the faces
E
of
P
instead of dilation of the facets of
P
by a parameter
h_{\rho}
(
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
),
we next prove an analogue of Theorem
5.1
for faces of a polytope.

Assume
P
is a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
with associated toric variety
X=X_{P}
and inner normal fan
\Sigma=\Sigma_{P}
.
Let
P(h)
be the dilation of
P
with respect to the vector
h=(h_{\rho})_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}
with real entries indexed by the rays of
\Sigma
. So, if
P
is defined by inequalities of the form

\langle m,u_{\rho}\rangle+c_{\rho}\geq 0,

with
u_{\rho}
the ray generators and
c_{\rho}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, for each
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
, then
P(h)
is defined by inequalities

\langle m,u_{\rho}\rangle+c_{\rho}+h_{\rho}\geq 0,

for each
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
. Similarly, we consider the dilation
E(h)
of a fixed face
E
of
P
, and let
\sigma_{E}\in\Sigma
be the cone corresponding to
E
.

Theorem 7.12
.

In the above notations, we have

(257)

\begin{split}\int_{E(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm&={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P(h)})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle\\
&={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle(i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot e^{\sum_{\rho}h_{\rho}\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle}\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle\>.\end{split}

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
5.1
, with the following modifications. Instead of
{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}(X)
, we use
{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([(i_{E})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\sigma_{E}}}])
, which is given by formula (
217
), together with the corresponding equivariant Riemann-Roch formula of (
121
) which is used for the calculation of Riemann sums approximating the integral. The Lebesgue measure
dm
on
E(h)
is now normalized so that the unit cube in the lattice
Span(E_{0})\cap M
has volume
1
, with
E_{0}:=E-m_{0}
a translation of
E
by a vertex
m_{0}\in E
. As before,
\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
n
in
z
, but the additional factor
\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle
is homogeneous of degree
\hbox{\rm codim}\,(E)
, fitting with the use of the multiplication factor
\frac{1}{k^{\dim(E)}}
needed for the Riemann sums considered here. Finally, we have

(258)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{\hbox{\rm codim}\,(E)}\cdot\langle i^{*}_{\sigma}({\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([(i_{E})_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\sigma_{E}}}])),\frac{1}{k}\cdot z\rangle\right)={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle.

This follows from formula (
217
) by the same calculation as in the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
∎

By combining the results of Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
7.12
, we obtain as an application a formula which relates integration over faces with a differentiation with respect to the corresponding
h
’s.

Corollary 7.13
.

Let
f
be a polynomial on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

(259)

\int_{E(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\int_{P(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\>,

as analytic functions in
z
and
h
near zero. In particular,

(a)

(260)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{E(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}\>,

for
p(x_{\rho})
a polynomial in the variables
x_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
(or a convergent power series as in the context of Remark
5.13
).

(b)

(261)

p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{E(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}={\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}\cdot p(\frac{\partial}{\partial h})\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}\>,

for a formal power series
p(x_{\rho})
in the variables
x_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.

Proof.

For
f=1
, formula (
259
) follows by comparing the results of Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
7.12
. In the general case, we apply the operator
f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
to both sides of the formula obtained for
f=1
. In (b) we finally evaluate the result at
z=0
. By Corollary
5.3
, a formal power series (as opposed to a convergent one) can be used in (
261
).
∎

In particular,

(262)

\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho_{k}}}\int_{P(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\>=0

if the corresponding facets for different
\rho_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,k
) do not intersect.

As another application of Corollary
7.13
, we can mention the following reformulation of the classical Euler-Maclaurin formula
(
204
) of Brion-Vergne (using (
35
)), for
f
a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
(see also
[
34
]
[page 22]):

(263)

\begin{split}&\sum_{m\in P\cap M}f(m)=\sum_{E\preceq P}\sum_{g\in G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\circ}}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}{1-a_{\rho}(g)e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}\left(\int_{P(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}\\
&=\sum_{E\preceq P}\frac{1}{{\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})}\sum_{g\in G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\circ}}\prod_{\rho\not\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}{1-e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}\frac{1}{1-a_{\rho}(g)\cdot e^{-\frac{\partial}{\partial h_{\rho}}}}\left(\int_{E(h)}f(m)\ dm\right)_{|_{h=0}}\>.\end{split}

The Euler-Maclaurin formula using the last operator is due to Guillemin
[
31
]
[Thm.1.3 and Eqn.3.28]

As a further application of Theorem
7.12
, we give an algebro-geometric proof of the
Stokes’ formula for polytopes

(see also
[
34
]
[Prop.6.1]).

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
be a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope, with corresponding inner fan
\Sigma
. Let
E_{\rho}
denote the facet of
P
corresponding to the ray
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.
For a fixed
m_{0}\in M
, let
\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{0}}
be the differentiation in direction of
m_{0}
. Then,

(264)

\int_{P(h)}\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{0}}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm=-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot\int_{E_{\rho}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm.

Proof.

Recall that

s(m_{0})=-c^{1}_{\mathbb{T}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\chi^{m_{0}}})=-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot F_{\rho}\in H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Then

\begin{split}\int_{P(h)}\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{0}}e^{\langle m_{,}z\rangle}\ dm&=\int_{P(h)}\langle m_{0},z\rangle\cdot e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm\\
&\overset{\eqref{f113}}{=}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P(h)})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle m_{0},z\rangle\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P(h)})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}s(m_{0}),z\rangle\\
&=-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma(n)}\frac{e^{\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}c^{\mathbb{T}}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D_{P(h)})),z\rangle}}{\langle Eu^{\mathbb{T}}_{X}(x_{\sigma}),z\rangle}\cdot\langle i_{\sigma}^{*}F_{\rho},z\rangle\\
&\overset{\eqref{f113b}}{=}-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot\int_{E_{\rho}(h)}e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm,\end{split}

as desired.
∎

Applying the operator
f(\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
to formula (
264
) for
f
a polynomial function on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we get the following:

(265)

\int_{P(h)}\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{0}}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm=-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot\int_{E_{\rho}(h)}f(m)e^{\langle m,z\rangle}\ dm

as analytic functions in
h
and
z
near zero. Further evaluation at
z=0
yields the identity

(266)

\int_{P(h)}\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{0}}f(m)\ dm=-\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma(1)}\langle m_{0},u_{\rho}\rangle\cdot\int_{E_{\rho}(h)}f(m)\ dm,

as polynomial functions in
h
near zero.

Remark 7.15
.

By Remark
7.3
, there is a surjection
{\mathcal{A}}(P)\to\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
, as
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
-modules, with
{\mathcal{A}}(P)
generated by the equivalence classes of
[V_{E}]\in{\mathcal{A}}(P)
, which are mapped to the equivariant fundamental classes
[V_{\sigma_{E}}]_{\mathbb{T}}
of orbit closures corresponding to all faces
E
of
P
. Hence,
\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
is generated as a
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module by the equivariant fundamental classes

[V_{\sigma_{E}}]_{\mathbb{T}}
.

Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose
elements

p_{\sigma_{E}}(t_{i})\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(pt;{\mathbb{Q}})=(\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{T}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq{\mathbb{Q}}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]]

with

(267)

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}])=\sum_{E\preceq P}\>p_{\sigma_{E}}(t_{i})[V_{\sigma_{E}}]_{\mathbb{T}}\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\>.

Then

{\rm td}_{*}^{\mathbb{T}}([{\mathcal{F}}])=p(s(m_{i}),F_{\rho})\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}),

for

(268)

p(t_{i},x_{\rho}):=\sum_{E\preceq P}\>\left({\rm mult}(\sigma_{E})\cdot\prod_{\rho\in\sigma_{E}(1)}x_{\rho}\right)\cdot p_{\sigma_{E}}(t_{i})\in{\mathbb{Q}}[[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x_{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Sigma(1)]]\>.

Altogether, with these notations, we get our third and final abstract Euler-Maclaurin formula coming from the equivariant Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch theorem, which provides a generalization of the Cappell-Shaneson Euler-Maclaurin formula (see Remark
7.17
below).

Theorem 7.16
.

Let
X=X_{P}
be the projective simplicial toric variety associated to a full-dimensional simple lattice polytope
P\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{P}
be the inner normal fan of
P
, and
D:=D_{P}
the ample Cartier divisor associated to
P
. Let
[{\mathcal{F}}]\in K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(X)
be fixed, and choose the formal power series
